```yaml
    description: The rules a new password keeps, each marked once the password typed keeps it.
    list: password-rule-list
    itemState: met
    items:
      length: 10 to 35 characters.
      digit: At least one digit.
```

The new password of `domain/screens/reset-password.yaml` opts in, and its screen gets `user.reset-password.password-rule-list`, `user.reset-password.password-rule-list.length-item` and `user.reset-password.password-rule-list.digit-item`, none of them written:

```yaml
user.reset-password.password-input:
  kind: input
  secret: true
  composite: password-rules
  binding:
    action: platform.reset-password
    params:
      password: user.reset-password.password-input
```

A composite's anchors are declared anchors the loader derives, not derived parts (`user.settings.password-rule-list.digit-item` is screen `user.settings`, kind `item`); they follow the anchor grammar, and no screen file writes them. The derived list and items are declared anchors like any other: the flow's guards, the scenarios, the tests and the sources name them as they name any. An unknown composite, an opt-in on anything but a secret input, a second opt-in of one composite on a screen, a written anchor whose id a composite derives, two composites deriving one anchor and a `derivedFrom` a screen file writes are problems. The platform's composites and the rows of the vocabulary's "Composites" table name each other, each item state a row state: the table names each composite with what it is for. The manifest lists them in `contract.composites`, the declarations under `platform.composites`, a secret input's `composite` and `derivedFrom` on the anchors a composite derives (`{ composite }` on its list, `{ composite, item }` on an item), and `contract:diff` reports a composite added or removed. A composite declared and used by no screen is no problem: `contract:coverage` notes it (`composite <name> is declared and used by no screen`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.composite`](#screen-anchors-name-composite), [`platform.composites`](#platform-composites), [`platform.composites.<name>`](#platform-composites-name).

### What an element's kind implies

<a id="rule-kind-parts"></a>
From version 4 an anchor's kind says what a file would only repeat, and the loader derives it: a `select` carries a `data-value` on each choice (`dataValue: true`) and renders its `option`s, a secret `input` renders its `visibility-button`, and a `dialog` and a `confirmation` render their two answers, `confirm-button` and `cancel-button`. Each part is listed in the anchor's `derived`, once, after the suffixes the file writes, so every reader of `derived` and `dataValue` (the checks, the manifest, the hand-over diff, the sources check) takes the anchor as if the file wrote them.

A screen file never writes one of them: `dataValue` on a select, whatever its value, and each of `option`, `visibility-button`, `confirm-button` and `cancel-button` that the kind implies, is a problem at its key (derived from its kind; leave it out). Before version 4 nothing is derived and a file writes them as before. A paged list derives its own parts by its facet (see the rule `paged-list`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.derived`](#screen-anchors-name-derived), [`screen.anchors.<name>.dataValue`](#screen-anchors-name-datavalue).

### The roles of a link

<a id="rule-link-roles"></a>
A link bound to one route (`binding: { route: /me }`) is shown to the devices that route opens. From version 4 the loader gives it the `roles` of the route's binding when the route admits fewer devices than the link's own screen serves (the frame serves every role). A link to a route that admits every device its screen serves, a link of several bindings and a link to a route no screen binds get none.

Roles are read by the devices they admit, never as sets of names: a device holds `guest` alone, or `identity` with any of the other roles, so `[identity, member, admin]` and `[identity]` admit the same devices (`sameAdmission` of `@workspace/ui-contract/anchors`, over the roles the bindings of the screens serve). A screen file never writes the roles the route derives: written roles that admit exactly what the route's do are a problem at the key (derived from the route; leave them out), and roles that admit fewer devices stay as the link's own. Before version 4 nothing is derived and a file writes them as before. `contract:check` reads the loaded roles as it reads written ones (see the rule `roles`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.roles`](#screen-anchors-name-roles).

### The search parameter of a mail's link

<a id="rule-mail-link-search"></a>
A platform action whose `mail` links to a screen names the search parameter that carries the token (`link: { screen: user.verify-email, search: token }`). From version 4 the loader gives every route of that screen that parameter, built from the platform value of the same name (`values.token`): its `type`, `enum`, bounds, `pattern`, `description` and `examples`. A screen file never writes it: a parameter of that name in a route's `search` is a problem at its key (derived from the mail link; leave it out). A mail whose `search` names no value of the platform, or a value a route cannot take (one without a `type`, or with a `format`), is a problem at its link. Before version 4 nothing is derived and a file writes the parameter as before.

The parameter is the screen's key (see the rule `address-key`) when an action of the screen takes it: the first action the screen runs with `{ search: <name> }`, an invoke of its flow before a source of a list, gives the first of the `codes` of the parameter that takes it, so the `token` of `user.verify-email` carries `x-perch: { key: link-invalid }`, the first code `platform.verify-email` lists for its `token`. A parameter that lists no `codes`, and an action of the model, give no key.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.bindings.search.properties.<name>`](#screen-bindings-search-properties-name), [`platform.actions.<name>.mail.link.search`](#platform-actions-name-mail-link-search).

### A description the engine can say

<a id="rule-engine-descriptions"></a>
An anchor's `description` may be left out where the engine says it: a `heading` names its screen by its title; a `link` whose one binding is a route to a screen's path leads to that screen; a secret `input` holds a secret the user types and never shows; an `error` says why the actions bound beside it, then those its flow invokes against it, did not go through, each of its codes with its meaning from the vocabulary's "Codes" table, the one place a code's meaning lives; and a composite's anchors carry the platform's descriptions. The manifest carries the written description, a secret input's with `(type password)` appended once, else the derived one, and gives each derived part in `contract.derivedParts` the meaning of its suffix. `bun run contract:coverage --descriptions` lists the written descriptions the engine would say itself, for the developer to drop (an anchor a composite derives is never listed).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.description`](#screen-anchors-name-description).

### What stays in a screen

<a id="rule-what-stays"></a>
What a screen would only repeat of the model, the platform and its own bindings, it leaves out, and the loader derives it, the same way every time. What stays in a screen is what a tester or a user can tell apart: the areas and their states, where each control leads, a success or a failure that goes elsewhere than the derived one or shows something else, guards, confirmations and chains of actions. A written value wins over a derived one. `bun run contract:diff <ref>` lists the codes and the error fields the anchors gain or lose, written or derived, so a constraint added to the model shows on review as the codes it adds, and the lines of each screen's flow, its states' addresses, what they render and what each guard by failure code takes among them, and every line of a flow added to or removed from a screen, descriptions aside, so a screen written shorter that loads to the same flow shows no change but the descriptions it stops writing, each listed as `<n> -> gone`.

### A password pair

<a id="rule-password-pair"></a>
A form that sets a new password asks for it twice: the new password, a secret `input` that opts into a composite (`composite: password-rules`), and its confirmation, a secret `input` with no binding, never sent, that names it: `confirms: user.reset-password.password-input`. The two are the screen's password pair, one per screen. The confirmation takes the value of the input it confirms (`fieldValueOf`; see the rule `declared-values`), so it is held to the same limits: where the password's value has a `maxLength`, the confirmation's element carries it too.

The loader derives the guards of the pair, on the parsed screen, each under the name a flow uses (`derive/pairs.ts`):

- `passwordBreaksRules`, `{ composite: breaks }`: the input the confirmation confirms breaks one of the rules of its composite's list;
- `passwordsDiffer`, `{ confirms: differs }`: the confirmation is not the same as the input it confirms;
- `confirmationStrays`, `{ confirms: strays }`: the confirmation is no longer how the input it confirms begins: a character differs, or it is longer;
- `confirmationFollows`, `{ confirms: follows }`: the confirmation is how the input it confirms begins, all of it or a part, or is empty.

Each is added to the flow's `meta.guards` only when a transition of the flow names it, so every guard declared stays used. The checks, the walk in XState, the manifest and an app's machine read it as any guard, by its name, and the app implements it. `contract:diff` lists each as `guard <name>: confirms <differs|strays|follows>` or `guard <name>: composite breaks`.

Refused, each at its key: a `confirms` on anything but a secret input, on an input with a binding, or naming anything but another secret input of its screen with a composite; a second `confirms` on a screen, the first making the pair; and a guard of a pair a screen file writes, `{ confirms }` or `{ composite }`, at every version, since only the loader derives one. From version 4 a screen with a pair never writes one of the four names either: a guard written under one of them beside `confirms` is a problem (derived from the confirms; leave it out). Before version 4 a name the screen writes keeps its written guard, and the others are derived. A screen without a `confirms` writes its guards as sentences, these four names included.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.confirms`](#screen-anchors-name-confirms), [`screen.flow.meta.guards`](#screen-flow-meta-guards).

### What a failure shows

<a id="rule-failure-show"></a>
In the `onError` of an invoke, `show: <anchor>` shows one of the codes the failure of the invoked action shows. In an `onError` list with a branch guarded by failure code, a bare `show` on that branch shows the guard's codes, and one on a later branch, unguarded or guarded by a sentence, the failure's codes but those the branches guarded by code before it take; a branch before the first one guarded by code shows the whole failure, and a `show` that names its codes keeps them. So where the state `signing-in` of `user.sign-in` writes `show: user.sign-in.error` on both of its branches, with `emailNotVerified: { codes: [ email-not-verified ] }`, the first shows `email-not-verified` and the second `invalid-credentials`, `rate-limited`, `other-account` and `failed`; a screen may write the codes out instead, and what it writes wins. The error's own codes are not narrowed: it carries every code of the failure, whichever branch shows it. A bare `show` after branches guarded by code that take every code of the failure is refused: that branch names the codes it shows.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.on.<name>.guard`](#screen-flow-states-name-on-name-guard), [`screen.flow.meta.guards.<name>.codes`](#screen-flow-meta-guards-name-codes).

### The outcome of a request

<a id="rule-request-outcome"></a>
An invoke whose action fails with codes and that writes no `onError` follows the pattern of a request: entering its state clears the error its failure shows on (unless the state's `entry` clears it already), and a failure goes back the one way it came, showing its codes on that error. The way back is the one sibling state whose transitions on controls enter the invoking state: only a control makes one, so a state a platform event or a data event enters has none. The error is `<part>.error` of the part those controls sit in (of the fields the invoke's `input` names, when no control enters it), else `<screen>.error`; with no part at all, no control entering it and its `input` naming no field (a request a link starts, its input a search parameter), it is `<screen>.error`.

So the state `changing` of `user.settings` may leave out its `entry` and its `onError`: the loader clears `user.settings.password.error` on entry and adds the `onError` back to `kept`, the one state whose `user.settings.password.save-button` enters it, showing the failure of `platform.change-password` on that error. An `onError` written as its target alone (a `description` beside it changes nothing) keeps that target and still gets the show, with no clear on entry; one with actions, or a guarded list (the state `resetting` of `user.reset-password`), is written in full and wins.

A state with no single way back writes its `onError`, and the check names the reason when it does not: one where its flow or its region starts (even when a sibling enters it too), one two states enter, one an invoke's outcome enters, one a platform event or a data event enters, one entered from outside its area. So does a state whose failure finds no error, its target alone written or not. A state with no way back may write its `onError` as its target alone, which still gets the show: a request a link starts where its flow starts writes `onError: <state>`, and its failure shows on `<screen>.error`. An action that fails with no code gets nothing.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.invoke.onError`](#screen-flow-states-name-invoke-onerror), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.invoke.onDone`](#screen-flow-states-name-invoke-ondone), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.entry`](#screen-flow-states-name-entry).

### The region of a button

<a id="rule-button-region"></a>
A button bound to one action that no state of the flow handles gets its region, when the root of the flow has one parallel state. The region is named by the button without its screen and its kind (`organisation.notes.add-button` gives `notes-add`) and comes after the regions written; it counts among the two regions or more of the parallel state, which may write fewer. It rests in `idle`; the button moves it to `running`, which invokes the action with the parameters the binding passes, and both outcomes lead back to `idle`. A failure shows its codes on the error of the button's part; the success of an action of the model says what the controls do by the word its name starts with: `create` (`createNote`), the controls whose values it took are emptied; `update`, they show the stored value; `delete`, the entry leaves its list. A failure with codes and no error in the part is a problem: the screen declares the error, or writes the region by its button with its `onError`.

A screen writes the region by its button, `by: <button>`, when it decides something of it: its `description`, `onDone` and `onError`, and the `toast: false` or `read: false` it says of its action, which the loader puts on the region's invoke; a branch of its `onDone` or `onError` that says `read: false` keeps it. An outcome left out is the derived one; a transition without a target goes back to `idle`. The states of a button's region are the machine's, named by the loader (`idle`, `running`), and a scenario or a test reads them as it reads any state.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.states.<name>.by`](#screen-flow-states-name-states-name-by).

### The toast of a success

<a id="rule-success-toast"></a>
With a toast anchor on the frame (`app-shell.toast`), every mutation says its success there, with a standard message: each invoke of an action of the model, written or in a derived region, gets on each branch of its `onDone` `show: { app-shell.toast: [<code>] }`, the code being the action's object in kebab case and the past of its verb (`note.createNote` gives `note-created`, `profile.updateBio` gives `bio-updated`), or `<action>-done` for a verb other than create, update and delete; a platform action toasts when `platform.yaml` lists it under `toasts`, with the code listed (`organisations.create: organisation-created`), and an unlisted one never does.

The toast says an operation's outcome once, where it ends: an operation starts with an event that enters a state running such an action and goes on along the branches of its `onDone` that enter another (one Save running `updateName`, then `updateBio`); such a step toasts nothing, and a branch that targets its own state re-enters nothing. An operation of one action says that action's code; one of several says the operation, `<object>-<verb>ed` from the control whose event starts it: the verb is the control's name without its kind, in the past (`save-button` gives `saved`), the object the part the control sits in, or the screen's last segment for a control of the screen itself (`user.profile.save-button` gives `profile-saved`). An operation of several that no control starts (a platform event), or that controls with different codes start, is refused until the screen writes its own message on the toast or bans it; so is one event that starts several such operations at once (the regions of a parallel state it enters, or two regions handling one press with transitions that stay in them), until the screen writes `toast: false` on every action of all but one. One press gives one toast.

The toast stays on unless the screen says otherwise: a branch of the `onDone` that shows its own message on the toast keeps it (a written message wins on its anchor, branch by branch; the other branches still get the standard one), and the one ban is `toast: false` on the invoke, or on a region written `by` its button (the language select's store: the change is visible by itself); a notice or an error written on the outcome says nothing about the toast. `read: false` sits beside it on the same invoke or region, a different statement: the action changes nothing a list of the screen shows, so nothing reads after it; on one branch of the `onDone`, nothing reads after that outcome, and the branch keeps its toast (see the rule `read-after-an-action`). A failure never toasts: it shows on the part's error.

The toast anchor carries every code a flow shows on it; the vocabulary documents the derived ones by one rule row, `<object>-<verb>ed`, the manifest lists each with its action, and `contract:diff` reports a new one. A code a flow writes on the toast itself (a warning of its own), or the frame lists on it, has its own row, even where a derivation gives the same code. The rule row is no code itself: the sources and consumers checks take a toast code the loader derived, never the row, and only the words of a derived toast code are written, in the frame's map.

Keys it concerns: [`platform.toasts`](#platform-toasts), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.invoke.toast`](#screen-flow-states-name-invoke-toast).

### The question before a destructive action

<a id="rule-destructive-question"></a>
With a confirmation on the frame (an anchor of kind `confirmation`, `app-shell.confirmation`), every control whose binding names a destructive action asks it before the action runs: an action of the model whose verb is `delete`, an action of the platform whose verb (the first word of its last segment) is `ban`, `delete`, `remove` or `revoke`. The manifest writes it on the control, `asks: { dialog, code }`, the code `<object>-<verb>` for the model (`note.deleteNote` gives `note-delete`) or the platform action's name with `.` as `-` (`organisations.remove` gives `organisations-remove`); the confirmation carries every code asked with, which the vocabulary documents by one rule row, `<object>-<verb>`. Both are derived only: a screen file never writes `asks`, nor the frame the confirmation's codes.

The flow says nothing of it: the press still leads to the state that runs the action, and the app's gate asks in front of the event, its confirm-button letting the event through and its cancel-button dropping it. The one line a screen writes is `confirm: false` on the control, which runs the action without asking; on a control that runs no destructive action it bans nothing and is refused. Without a confirmation on the frame nothing asks. The frame's confirmation is declared by the frame alone, once, reserved, with both answers and no action of its own.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.confirm`](#screen-anchors-name-confirm).

### Scenario files and their ids

<a id="rule-scenario-files"></a>
A scenario is an instruction a person could follow by hand, one Markdown file each, in the e2e tree beside its test:

```text
tests/e2e/scenarios/<entity>/<layer>/[<area>/]<slug>.md
tests/e2e/src/<entity>/<layer>/[<area>/]<slug>.e2e.spec.ts
```

The id is the file's path under `tests/e2e/scenarios/` without `.md`, never written inside the file: `user/base/edit-profile-name`. Its segments go from the general to the specific:

- the entity the scenario checks: `user`, `device`, `organisation`, and `contract` for the contract itself;
- the layer: `base` (the entity works on its own; an area such as `crud` or `list` when it helps), `flows` (several roles or devices meet) or `features` (how one function behaves);
- an optional area, then a kebab-case slug.

No numbers: the tree orders the scenarios and the tags select them. The role is a property of the scenario, in its front matter, never a folder. A path that does not match `^[a-z][a-z0-9-]*(\/[a-z][a-z0-9-]*){2,3}$`, or whose layer is not one of the three, is a problem of its file. The scenarios are sorted by id, an id before the ids that extend it (`user/base/sign-up` before `user/base/sign-up-taken-email`).

### The header and the body of a scenario

<a id="rule-scenario-format"></a>
The file opens with a YAML front matter that holds only the structured header:

- `tags`: a list holding `smoke` or `full`;
- `activity`, when the scenario walks one: the id of an activity of `contract/domain/activities/`;
- `devices`: a letter per browser with the role it acts as by the end of the scenario; every device starts as `guest` until a step signs it in;
- `data`: the test data by name, `generate` or a fixed string; `{}` when there is none.

Then the body, with fixed sections in any order: `# <title>`, a line `Where: <text>`, `## Scenario: <sentence>`, `### Steps` with a numbered list and `### Expected result` with a bullet list. An unknown heading, a line outside a section, a missing section or a list item of the wrong shape is a problem, with a line number where the parser knows it (a missing section has none). The whole of `tests/e2e/scenarios/user/base/edit-profile-name.md`:

```markdown
---
tags:
  - smoke
devices:
  A: identity
data:
  New name: generate
---
# Rename yourself on the profile page

Where: [/me] then [user.profile.save-button]

## Scenario: A signed-in user changes their name and keeps it after a reload

### Steps
1. Open [/me] and wait for [app-shell.status] to reach [state:ready]
2. Clear [user.profile.name-input]
3. Enter the new name into [user.profile.name-input]
4. Click [user.profile.save-button]

### Expected result
- After step 4 [app-shell.notice-message] carries [code:profile-saved]
- [user.profile.heading] shows the new name
- Reloading the page keeps the new name
```

A scenario that walks an activity names it in its front matter (`activity: session`), and the coverage note counts, per activity, the scenarios that name it and the transitions they walk. `parseScenario(id, markdown)` reads one into a `Scenario` with `lines`, the line of each key of the front matter (`devices.A`), each section (`where`) and each list item (`steps[0]`), or returns its problems with their lines; `formatScenario(scenario)` writes one, its inverse.

### The brackets of a scenario and what they pair with

<a id="rule-scenario-brackets"></a>
Brackets reference the contract, and the contract check resolves each one: `[<anchor>]` (a declared anchor, or one derived from a declared base with a suffix it lists), `[/path]` (the path of a binding), `[code:<code>]`, `[state:<state>]`, `[value:<value>]` and `[mail:<action>]` (a platform action that declares the mail it sends) and `[notice:<action>.<name>]` (a notice of that name the platform action declares). A value `show` or `hide` is one of the contract once an input renders a `visibility-button`.

In one text (the `Where` line, the scenario sentence, a step or an expected result), a `[code:…]`, a `[state:…]` or a `[value:…]` speaks of the anchor the text names last before it, and the check wants it to be one that anchor carries: a code of its codes, written or derived, a state of its `states`, a value of its choice values, its own or those of an anchor declared under it (`<anchor>.<rest>`: the items of a composite's list, the options of a select, the eye of an input). So `[user.sign-in.mail-message] carries [code:check-your-mail]` holds, and `every item of [user.sign-in.password-rule-list] carries [state:met]` holds through the list's items, while `[user.confirm-email.error] carries [code:welcome]` is a problem, `user.confirm-email.error never carries code welcome`.

A route is read past: in `wait for [app-shell.status] to reach [state:ready] on [/me]` the state is the status's. A `[mail:…]` or a `[notice:…]` ends the pairing until the text names an anchor again, since what follows speaks of the mail or the notice (its language, its link). With no anchor before it in its text, a code, a state or a value need only be one of the contract. Write the anchor first, then its code, its state or its value: a value written before its anchor pairs with an anchor the text named earlier, if any. `Choose [value:dark] in [user.settings.theme-select]` holds the value to the contract alone, and `[user.settings.theme-select] shows [value:dark]` holds it to the select. `referencesIn(text)` reads the bracket references of a text in the order it writes them, `anchorCarries(contract)` says whether an anchor carries a code, a state or a value (one it declares or one an anchor under it declares: codes are the loaded `codes`, derived ones included; states the anchor's `states`; values the rows of its choice values and its options', a pattern row matching, or `show` and `hide` on a `visibility-button`), and `pairingResolver(contract)` says why a value does not pair with its anchor, for the scenarios and the consumers check alike.

The check also holds each device to a role of the vocabulary, the activity a scenario names to exist, a mail to the platform action that declares it and a notice to its action and its name (`unknownNotice`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.codes`](#screen-anchors-name-codes), [`screen.anchors.<name>.states`](#screen-anchors-name-states), [`screen.anchors.<name>.dataValue`](#screen-anchors-name-datavalue).

### The test of a scenario, and the feedback loop

<a id="rule-scenario-tests"></a>
The test pairs with the scenario by its path and names it on its first line, `// scenario: user/base/edit-profile-name`, with one `test.step` per step. `bun run e2e:scaffold <id>` writes a failing test from a new scenario, and `bun run e2e:coverage` checks the pairs, step for step: it runs in `bun run check` and in the pre-commit hook on a commit on `main`, while on a branch the hook leaves it out, so a scenario may wait for its test. The test of the scenario locates by anchors and asserts codes, states and values, never wording.

The scenarios are the contract's feedback loop. A scenario is written from the contract alone: when it needs an anchor, a code, a state, a route or a role the contract lacks, that gap is fixed in the contract files first, never worked around in a test. A gap the contract cannot close in the change is signalled, never emulated.

### The three contract checks

<a id="rule-checks"></a>
`bun run check` and the pre-commit hook run three contract checks. Each prints `file:line: message` per problem and exits 1, or a one-line summary. A commit on `main` also checks that the contract is at the version the engine reads (`contract:check --current`). `bun run contract:check`: the files on their own and against each other (the rules `check-files`, `check-bindings`, `check-flows`, `check-platform` and `check-access`). `bun run contract:check:sources`: the JSX of `apps/web/src` and `packages/ui/src` against the contract (the rule `check-sources`). `bun run contract:check:consumers`: the e2e tests under `tests/e2e/src` against the contract (the rule `check-consumers`).

Beside them, `bun run e2e:coverage` pairs every scenario with its test, step for step: in `bun run check` and in the pre-commit hook on a commit on `main`, while on a branch the hook leaves it out, so a scenario may wait for its test. `bun run contract:coverage:check` runs where it runs and holds the block of `COVERAGE.md` to what `contract:coverage --write` writes. The web app's tests hold the module routes and the real router to the bindings, and XState and the app's machines to the flows. `checkContract(root)` loads the contract files, the activities and the scenarios (stopping at their own problems), then runs the cross-checks, `schemaDrift` and `referenceDrift`, sorted by file and line; each rule is a function of the loaded `Contract`.

`bun run contract:migrate` moves a contract at an older version to the tooling's, step by step (`--write` rewrites the files; `packages/ui-contract/MIGRATIONS.md` says what each version changed); `contract:check` passes an older contract and names it in one line (see the rule `editions-and-migrations`).

### The check of the files and the vocabulary

<a id="rule-check-files"></a>
Each file parses and holds its shape, and each screen file is named by its first binding. Every anchor follows the grammar, starts with its screen's id and is declared once, and is never named like another anchor plus a derived suffix. Every kind, suffix, state, code, role and choice value is in the vocabulary, every derived suffix ends with a kind of it (the kind of the part it marks), and every code of the vocabulary is used; there are no choice values for a `visibility-button`, whose values its suffix defines. The platform's composites and the rows of the vocabulary's "Composites" table name each other, each item state a row state. The version is one the tooling reads (`versionProblems`): at most `CONTRACT_VERSION`, and from version 2 declared in `platform.yaml` alone, the vocabulary repeating it at version 1 only (its `Contract version` line is there and equal at version 1, refused from version 2). The generated schemas equal their Zod sources, and so does the reference (`schemaDrift`, `referenceDrift`), and each link of a guide into the reference, `REFERENCE.md#<slug>` in the files of `GUIDE_FILES` (the folder's guide, the rule, the skill, the manifest's instructions, the package's guide and the vocabulary), names an anchor of it (`referenceLinkProblems`).

`secret` is on a field alone, and a `visibility-button` on a secret input alone; `marksState` (`false`) is on an `input`, a `textarea` or a `select` that holds input alone; `ranked` is on a message of two codes or more alone, each code listed once; a `composite` is on a secret input alone, names a composite of the platform, is opted into once per screen and derives no anchor the file writes or another composite derives, and no file writes `derivedFrom`.

Every description the domain files write, the maps of meanings included (a screen's `states`, the platform's `events`, the sentence guards of a `meta.guards`), says what its element is for and leaves to the structure, another element, the platform, the model, the vocabulary and the flow what they say, as the schema text of its field puts it; each keeps 200 characters at most, whatever it describes (`descriptionsProblems`): one past it is a problem at its line, `<path>: the description of <place> is <n> characters, past its budget of 200; its purpose: "<the purpose of its field>"`, naming the purpose of its field. `DESCRIPTION_PURPOSES` gives, by its `DescriptionKind`, the purpose of each description field in one text (what it is for, what it leaves to others and its budget), which is that field's `.describe()` in the schemas, so it reaches the generated JSON Schemas as an editor's hint, the model's own description taking its text in the shape `loadModel` reads the document with; `DESCRIPTION_BUDGET` is the 200 characters, for every one; `descriptionLength(text)` counts a description in characters. `readDescriptions(root)` reads every description the domain files write, as they write them, never one the engine derives or the loader fills in as it expands a shorter form.

Every activity agrees with the screens it visits (`activitiesProblems`): every state's screen exists and its address is a binding of that screen; every event is a declared anchor (a derived one through its base's `derived`, never a `visibility-button`) of that screen or of the frame; every transition from a state on an anchor to a target is backed by the screens, the flow of the anchor's screen (the frame's, for an anchor of the frame) reaching a `navigate` to the target's path, in the transition's actions, on leaving its state or entering its target (the `exit` and `entry` effects XState runs on a move) or after an invoke's `onDone` or `onError`, or the anchor being a link to that path (`binding: { route: ... }`); the roles are of the vocabulary and every `data` model is a field of the model; `initial` and every target are states; every state is reachable from `initial` and every state that is not final leaves; every guard is declared in `meta.guards` and every declared one is used. `contract:check` builds each activity in XState as written, with the walk of the flows, reaches every state and takes every transition and every guarded branch to its target (`runsProblems`: a machine XState will not build is a problem, never a crash). Every bracket reference of a scenario resolves, each code, state or value one the anchor named last before it in its text carries (see the rule `scenario-brackets`), the activity it names exists, and every device acts as a role of the vocabulary. A problem of an activity or a scenario is at the line of the deepest part of its path the file holds.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.secret`](#screen-anchors-name-secret), [`screen.anchors.<name>.ranked`](#screen-anchors-name-ranked), [`screen.anchors.<name>.marksState`](#screen-anchors-name-marksstate).

### The check of the bindings and the routes

<a id="rule-check-bindings"></a>
Every binding points at a field, an action or a route that exists, or at a search parameter every route of the screen declares, which only a field that holds no secret starts with, and never on another kind than a field (`bindingsProblems`). Each parameter of an action takes a declared anchor (never a `visibility-button`, which holds no input), a constant or a search parameter, an item or a row of its own screen with a `dataId`, of a list, a table or a section, from a control (a button, a link, a tab or a field) in its part (in an invoke's `input`, a state only such controls enter); a parameter two fields of a button's part pass with different values is written by the button. Each param of a platform source is a parameter of its action taking a constant or a search parameter every route of the screen declares, never `page` or `size`.

A paged source's action pages with its sizes each once, the smallest first, its default one of them, and takes the number parameters `page` and `size`; every route of its screen declares `page` and `size` as it reads them; a screen pages one list; and a paged list has at most 3 segments, as its size options add two to its name (`pagingProblems`). Every path parses in the router's syntax (`parseRoutePath`), every example of a route parameter matches that parameter (Ajv's reasons at `bindings[i].params.properties.<name>.examples[j]`), an empty example of a path parameter is a problem, and a `$param` without an example has a value its schema accepts, else `needs an example` at its property (`routesProblems`, `searchProblems`: the enum controls, save the `size` of a screen with a paged list, whose control is the derived `<list>.size-select.option` and which writes no `x-perch`; `retain`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.binding`](#screen-anchors-name-binding), [`screen.bindings`](#screen-bindings).

### The check of the flows

<a id="rule-check-flows"></a>
Every flow names states, events (never a `visibility-button`, which is presentation only), guards, actions, and anchors with their codes and bindings that exist (`flowProblems`): a clear's anchor one that carries codes, which then shows none, whatever its kind and screen, else a field of the flow's own screen, which it empties (a secret one included, never a `visibility-button`); a read's anchor a list, section or table of the flow's own screen whose `source` is a platform action, never one with an entity source or none (`flow/reads.ts`); an invoke's `input` taken from no `visibility-button` either; an invoke that says `read: false` reading in none of its outcomes, and a branch that says `read: false` an outcome of an invoke, never the transition of an event, reading nothing itself, under no invoke that says it already; every branch of an invoke's `onDone`, unless the invoke says `read: false`, reading each list, section or table of its own screen with a platform source that the invoke holds (one whose entry its `input` takes, whose source's `params` take a value its `input` takes, or whose source its action's `changes` names; `containersOf`, `flow/held-reads.ts`), or saying `read: false` itself (see the rule `read-after-an-action`).

A navigate's search keys are keys of the binding it moves to, each `{ anchor }` a field of the flow's own screen that holds no secret (never a `visibility-button`), each `{ item }` an entry with a `dataId` of a list, a table or a section of the flow's own screen, taken on the event of a control (`CONTROL_KINDS`) in its part into a key that takes a string (`flow/items.ts`), and `page: 1` beside any key a paged list of the screen it moves to reads (`flow/pages.ts`); an invoke's input that takes an entry is run in a state only such controls enter. Each navigate sits on a transition, one to a transition, a control's on a control of its own screen outside the frame (a derived part within its screen only), to its own path only on a screen of one binding, and leaving its screen, losing the same fields, everywhere its control is handled or nowhere (`flow/leaves.ts`).

Every target resolves as XState does (a state of the root or a deep target `<parallel>.<region>.<state>` from the root, a state of its own region or an id target `#<flow id>.<state>` of its own flow in a region); each event of a parallel state is handled in one area; the flow reaches each of its states and leaves each plain state that is not final; the one transition without a guard comes last. `meta.renders` lists anchors its own screen declares (a derived part's base, never the part), each once and never beside an anchor it is declared under; a plain state of the root or a state of a region renders every anchor it handles that a state lists (a derived part, or an anchor declared under a listed one, through the anchor it is under). A guard by failure code guards only branches of an invoke's `onError`, each of its codes one its action fails with and listed once; and every invoke whose action fails with codes writes its `onError` in full or has what the request pattern needs: the one way back and an error when it writes none, an error when it writes its target alone (see the rule `request-outcome`). `flowNodes(flow)` lists every state in the order of the file as a `FlowNode` (a `RootNode`, `ParallelNode`, `RegionNode` or `RegionStateNode`, with its path of names and its place in the flow); `deepTargetOf`, `idTargetOf`, `rootOf`, `regionStartsOf` and `deepEnteredOf` read the parts of a `DeepTarget` and an `IdTarget`: the state of the root a target lands in, each region at its starting state.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow`](#screen-flow), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.invoke`](#screen-flow-states-name-invoke), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.on`](#screen-flow-states-name-on).

### The check of the platform file

<a id="rule-check-platform"></a>
Every action of `leaving` exists and is listed once, every action of `toasts` is an action of the platform (`leavingProblems`, `toastsProblems`), the frame's confirmation is declared by the frame alone, once, reserved, with both answers and no action of its own, and `leaving` comes with `unsavedInput`, whose question is a reserved dialog with a code and both answers that runs no action of its own, and an action that asks needs an action under `leaving` (`unsavedInputProblems`).

Every mail a platform action declares goes to a parameter of the action or the account, takes its language from a parameter, the account or, sent to a parameter by an action no guest runs, its recipient's, and links to a screen whose every route declares the search parameter that carries its token (`mailsProblems`); an action that sends a mail or a notice has no parameter named `account`, and one that sends a mail none named `recipient`. Scenarios name a mail `[mail:<action>]` and a notice `[notice:<action>.<name>]`, and a test holds a mail it reads to what the action declares. Every notice it declares has a name no other notice of the action has, goes and takes its language as a mail does, never its recipient's, and links, when it does, to a screen whose every route needs no parameter: no `$param`, no splat, no required search key (`noticesProblems`). `platformMailSchema` is the mail (`to` and `language`, each a parameter of the action or `ACCOUNT`, the account the action acts on: the signed-in account for an action on its own account, else the one its parameters name, whoever is signed in; the language also `RECIPIENT`, the account whose email the mail goes to, else the signed-in account's; and the `link` with the `screen` it opens and the `search` parameter carrying its token) and `platformNoticeSchema` a notice of the action's `notices`, a mail that only tells, with no token: its kebab-case `name`, `to` and `language` as a mail's, never `RECIPIENT`, `to: account` being the account's email as it was before the action changed anything, and an optional plain `link` to a `screen`, never a `search`.

Every action, of the platform or of the model, writes its `changes`, each name of which is a platform action, never the action itself, that a list, a section or a table of some screen takes as its `source`, written once; the schema refuses an action without them, as it refuses one without `codes` (`changesProblems`, a problem per name at its place in the platform file or the model).

Every parameter of a platform action writes its `type` or its `value`, and a `value` names a value of `platform.values` or a field of the model (`valueProblems`, a problem at the parameter; see the rule `declared-values`). From version 3 every string parameter is bounded and every code of a field concerns a parameter whose value has the constraint the code names (`attributionProblems`; see the rule `attributed-codes`).

Keys it concerns: [`platform.leaving`](#platform-leaving), [`platform.toasts`](#platform-toasts), [`platform.unsavedInput`](#platform-unsavedinput), [`platform.actions.<name>.changes`](#platform-actions-name-changes).

### The check of who a route serves

<a id="rule-check-access"></a>
No binding's `guard` sends away an account its `roles` name (`authenticated` with `guest`, or `guest` with any other role), an anchor's `roles` are among those its screen's bindings serve (the frame aside), a link to a route whose roles name neither `guest` nor `identity` is shown to no device the route refuses: every restriction above it (its own `roles`, those of each anchor it is declared under, and its screen's, the frame aside) admits a device by the roles it holds together, so one restriction naming only roles the route serves is enough, wherever it sits on the chain, and the binding at `/$` names `guest` and `identity` (`accessProblems`; see the rule `roles`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.bindings.roles`](#screen-bindings-roles), [`screen.anchors.<name>.roles`](#screen-anchors-name-roles).

### The check of the sources

<a id="rule-check-sources"></a>
`bun run contract:check:sources` holds the JSX of `apps/web/src` and `packages/ui/src` against the contract. `collectFromSource(text, file)` reads one file with oxc-parser and returns its `Collected` anchors (`data-testid`, `testId` and `*TestId` attributes, `testId:` keys, `<NAME>_TEST_ID` constants), the suffixes of its `testIdFor()` calls (each with its base when the call writes it as a literal), its codes (`data-code`, and the kebab-case values of `code:` keys and `<NAME>_CODES` maps; a domain code such as `note:TEXT_REQUIRED` is left out, a `data-code` outside the grammar is a problem) and an anchor built at runtime as a problem (`RUNTIME_ANCHOR`, `RUNTIME_SUFFIX`); `scanSources(root)` collects the tracked and untracked sources under `SOURCE_ROOTS` (`apps/web/src`, `packages/ui/src`) except tests, declarations, generated files, fixtures and snapshots.

`compareWithContract(collected, screens, vocabulary)` wants each literal written once, the literals equal to the anchors of the screen files (an anchor no source renders is a problem too), no literal equal to another plus a derived suffix, every `testIdFor()` suffix in the vocabulary and listed in the `derived` of an anchor of the contract (of the base itself, when the call writes it as a literal), so a part no anchor declares, a `visibility-button` no input lists, is never rendered, and every code a code of the contract: a row of the vocabulary, or a toast code the loader derived (the rule row `<object>-<verb>ed` is no code itself; `codesOf(vocabulary, screens)`).

A field the contract says holds no input (a choice that applies at once) carries `holdsInput={false}` on the element with its anchor, and a field the contract says drops its state colour carries `marksState={false}`; each flag is read as a boolean literal on the element that carries a field's anchor as a literal or as `testIdFor()` of two literals (an entry of the latter with its `base`, as a paged list's size select is built; a prop passed through is let by, any other value or place is `HOLDS_INPUT_VALUE`, `HOLDS_INPUT_ANCHOR`, `MARKS_STATE_VALUE` or `MARKS_STATE_ANCHOR`, and for `marksState` a wrapper that carries the anchor as `testId` is read too). A field that holds input carries nothing (`holdsInput={true}` is the default and is reported, and so is a `holdsInput={false}` on a field that holds input or on an anchor that is not a field), a `marksState` is written only as `{false}`, never on a kind that is not an `input`, a `textarea` or a `select` that holds input (a form, a paged list's size select included), and a rendered field that holds no input, or drops its colour, without its opt-out is reported at its anchor (except in ahead mode). The opt-outs sit in `sources/opt-outs.ts`: a paged list's size select, `<list>.size-select` of a declared anchor whose `derived` holds `size-select`, is a select that holds no input by its facet, so a `holdsInput={false}` on it is no problem, and a rendered one without it is reported at its `testIdFor()` (except in ahead mode), and a flag of a part a `testIdFor()` derives that is no size select is a problem.

Coverage markers are read by `ignoreMarkers(text, { program, comments })`: `ui-contract-ignore: <reason>` (the element and its subtree) or `ui-contract-ignore-self: <reason>` (the element alone), as a line comment in code (`// ui-contract-ignore: <reason>`) or as the only content of a JSX comment container among JSX children (`{/* ui-contract-ignore: <reason> */}`), with only white space before the element; each `IgnoreMarker` points at the element after it, and a marker whose own reason is shorter than three characters, or in another form, is a fault (`IGNORE_WITHOUT_REASON`, `IGNORE_MALFORMED`; `IGNORE_WITHOUT_ELEMENT` when no element starts where it points, `IGNORE_NO_CONTROL` when its element, and its subtree for the plain form, holds no control), which makes `contract:coverage` fail and write nothing.

On a branch the pre-commit hook runs the sources check as `contract:check:sources:ahead`, which lets the contract run ahead of the app (see the rule `ahead-mode`); a commit on `main` and `bun run check` take the full check.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.holdsInput`](#screen-anchors-name-holdsinput), [`screen.anchors.<name>.marksState`](#screen-anchors-name-marksstate).

### The check of the e2e tests

<a id="rule-check-consumers"></a>
`bun run contract:check:consumers` holds the e2e tests under `tests/e2e/src` against the contract. `collectFromTest(text, file)` reads one file with oxc-parser and returns what it names of the contract (`Consumed`, its `ConsumedLiteral`s): the anchor of a `getByTestId('<anchor>')` call; in any string or template literal the `[data-testid="..."]`, `[data-code="..."]`, `[data-state="..."]` and `[data-value="..."]` selectors, in double or single quotes; and the value a `toHaveAttribute('data-<testid|code|state|value>', '<value>')` assertion expects, `.not` included; each argument a plain string or a template literal without `${}`, or a `+` chain of them read as the one string it folds to (a TypeScript wrapper such as `as const` changes nothing), each selector at its line in the source. `scanConsumers(root, tests?)` collects the `.ts` files under `CONSUMER_ROOT` (`tests/e2e/src`) or the folder given.

`compareConsumers(consumed, contract)` wants every anchor known (declared, or derived from a declared base with a suffix it lists in `derived`), every code a code of the contract (a row of the vocabulary or a derived toast code), and every state and value in the vocabulary, or `show` and `hide` once an input renders a `visibility-button`, as the scenarios check resolves its bracket references. A code, a state or a value asserted on an anchor (`ConsumedLiteral.of`) must be one that anchor carries, as `anchorCarries` reads it, as in a scenario: in a selector string, `[data-testid="app-shell.leave-dialog"][data-code="unsaved-input"]`, the attribute selectors of one element pair with its `data-testid`, before or after it (`elementAnchors` in `elements.ts`), attribute selectors written one after another are of one element, and a `:not(`, `:is(` or `:where(` of one compound is that element too (a `data-testid` in a `:not(` naming none); a `:has(`, any other parenthesised argument, or a `:not(` holding a combinator or a list speaks of elements of its own, which pair among themselves, and after its `)` the element before it goes on; a space, a combinator, a list, an expression or other text leads to another element. A `toHaveAttribute('data-code', 'text-required')` pairs with the last `getByTestId('<anchor>')` of the receiver of its `expect(...)` (`assertedAnchor` in `receiver.ts`), `page.getByTestId('organisation.notes.error')`, when every step after it narrows what it found (`first`, `last`, `nth`, `filter`, `and`). A receiver held in a variable or built by a helper pairs with nothing and is held to the contract alone.

A plain string or a template without `${}` is a literal; an anchor built with `${}` or held in a name is a runtime anchor and a problem (`RUNTIME_TEST_ANCHOR`), while a code, a state or a value from an expression is a parameter of the test and passes. A helper such as `notice(page, 'profile-saved')` is not followed, and a `data-id` is data, not contract.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.codes`](#screen-anchors-name-codes), [`screen.anchors.<name>.states`](#screen-anchors-name-states), [`screen.anchors.<name>.dataValue`](#screen-anchors-name-datavalue).

### Editions and migrations of a contract

<a id="rule-editions-and-migrations"></a>
A contract is read by the version its `platform.yaml` declares, `editionOf(contract)`, and a rule a version brings applies from that version on, `since(contract, N)` (the contract is at N or later), so a contract at an older version loads and checks as before: a rule of version N starts with `if (!since(contract, N)) return []`. `outdatedProblem(contract)` is a contract older than `CONTRACT_VERSION`, named with the command that moves it, or undefined: `contract:check` passes an older contract and names it in one line.

`migrate(root, { write, migrations?, current? })` runs each `Migration` (`from`, `note`, and `run(files)`, which edits the `MigrationFiles` in place, the platform and the screen files as YAML documents and the vocabulary as text, and returns the work left by hand) from the declared version up to `current`, `CONTRACT_VERSION` by default, from `migrations`, the registry `MIGRATIONS` (one per version, ordered by `from`) by default, setting `version` after each step. It returns the `MigrationReport` (`from`, `to`, and per step its `from`, `note`, the files it changed and the work by hand) and, with `write`, writes the files the steps changed, each document into the text it was read from (`files/write-yaml-document/`): only the nodes a step edited change (a scalar in place, a pair or an item by its lines, a flow collection whose parts changed as a whole), every other byte stays as written. A contract newer than `current`, a platform file without a version, a file that is not YAML and a version no migration reads throw. `parseYamlDocument(text)` is the `yaml` package's `Document` of a text, its comments, the style of every node and its range in the text kept. `packages/ui-contract/MIGRATIONS.md` says what each version changed.

### The business and the meta layer: three kinds of commit

<a id="rule-layers-and-commits"></a>
The contract has a business layer and a meta layer. The business layer is the entry point of every business feature: `contract/domain/` (the screens, the activities, the platform, the model), the scenarios of `tests/e2e/scenarios/`, and the content tables of `contract/vocabulary.md` (the codes, the states, the choice values, the screen names, the roles, the shell's reserved anchors): a row of them travels with the screen file that first uses it. The meta layer is the engine that keeps the contract working: `@workspace/ui-contract` (its schemas, checks, manifest, runtime helpers, and `./machines`, the walk that runs every flow and activity in XState inside `contract:check` and the comparison an app holds its own machines to their flows with), the generated `contract/schemas/` and `contract/REFERENCE.md`, the grammar of `contract/vocabulary.md` (its Grammar section, the kinds, the derived suffixes, the attributes), `contract/README.md`, the rules and skills about the contract, the contract steps of the pre-commit hook, the prose of `COVERAGE.md` and the contract's rows of `docs/agents/context-smoke-test.md`.

An app is one consumer of the engine, a particular case: its code and its tests are the implementation, its runtime of the contract (`window.__perch.ui`, the served manifest) and the specs that hold it to the contract included (the web app's `apps/web/src/platform/machines.spec.ts` with its `APP_MACHINES`, `routes-*.spec.ts`, the e2e spec of the manifest), and so is an example in a meta doc that quotes the app's code, which changes in the implementation commit that changes that code. A meta commit touches an app only where the app consumes what the engine changed (an import path, a call), never what the app does. On a branch a commit holds one of the three kinds, never two. A feature that needs a new facet lands the meta change first (the engine learns it), then commits the business change that uses it. The generated block of `COVERAGE.md` goes with whichever commit changed what it measures, on an area branch: it measures the code too, so the contract trunk leaves it to the implementation that follows the take; `contract:coverage:check` fails a block that is not what `contract:coverage --write` writes, in `bun run check` and in the hook on `main`.

The files are of two kinds. Authored: `contract/vocabulary.md`, `contract/domain/screens/<pathname>.yaml` (`__root.yaml` for the frame), `contract/domain/platform.yaml`, `contract/domain/model.schema.json`, `contract/domain/activities/<id>.yaml`. Generated, never edited by hand: `screen.schema.json`, `platform.schema.json`, `activity.schema.json`, `x-perch.schema.json`, `manifest.schema.json` and `contract/REFERENCE.md` (`bun run contract:schemas`) and the block of `contract/COVERAGE.md`. A word the vocabulary lacks is added to it in the same change. `isBusinessFile(path)` says whether a file is one of the business layer's (a domain file, the vocabulary or a scenario): the only files a commit on the contract trunk may stage.

### Ahead mode: the contract runs ahead of the app on a branch

<a id="rule-ahead-mode"></a>
On a branch the pre-commit hook runs the sources check as `contract:check:sources:ahead`, which lets the contract run ahead of the app: an anchor declared, not rendered yet, passes, and so does a field's opt-out (`holdsInput={false}`, `marksState={false}`), a size select's included, not written yet; the app uses only what the contract declares. A commit on `main` and `bun run check` take the full check, `e2e:coverage` and `contract:coverage:check` included, so nothing lands unrendered, half-built, untested or with its coverage block behind.

The same holds for the app's machines: on a branch the hook runs the unit tests with `CONTRACT_AHEAD=1`, and a machine may lack what its flow added (a state, an event, a guarded branch, an effect, an anchor a state renders) but does nothing the flow does not say, or is still exactly its screen's flow at the branch's merge base with `main` (`CONTRACT_AHEAD_BASE`; during a merge, the base the merge's commit will have, so a merge of `main` compares the app it brings with the flows it brings) or at the commit the branch is at (`CONTRACT_AHEAD_HEAD`, the contract the app was built from: a card's second business commit, taken onto a branch whose app already follows its first, changes a flow the base may not have at all; during a merge, the tip before it, unless the engine the merge brings cannot load that tip's contract for any reason, a key it now requires among them, and then that tip gives no flows to match), so a business commit may change a transition the app has not changed yet. `aheadOf(app, flow)` is the flow as far as a machine that lags it has come (equal to the machine's projection exactly when it does nothing the flow does not say) and `laggingOf(app, flow, base)` the flow at the branch's base when the machine is still exactly that, else `aheadOf`.

The app's routes likewise: a binding may wait for its route, and a route that still reads as its binding did at the merge base (the same screen, the same path) is held to that binding as it was there. A scenario may wait for its test, as the hook leaves `e2e:coverage` out on a branch. The hook names both commits, and the cache of the unit tests keys on them, so a pass never carries into another branch state with the same files. A branch whose merge base with a local `main` cannot be found fails the hook; on `main`, on a detached HEAD and in `bun run check` every machine is its whole flow. A contract at an older version, as the hook's ahead mode reads main's (`CONTRACT_AHEAD_BASE`) with the new engine, loads and checks as before (see the rule `editions-and-migrations`).

A rename or a removal keeps the old name declared until the implementation has switched to the new name or stopped rendering the removed element, then a last business commit drops it. It therefore goes in three commits: the business commit adds the new name and keeps the old one (on the trunk, or with the review stop on an area branch), the implementation switches to it, a last business commit on the area branch drops the old one, approved with the first. The ahead mode cannot drop a name the app still renders.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.holdsInput`](#screen-anchors-name-holdsinput), [`screen.anchors.<name>.marksState`](#screen-anchors-name-marksstate).

### The contract trunk holds a commit to the business layer

<a id="rule-trunk-commits"></a>
On `contract/trunk` the pre-commit hook first holds each commit to the business layer (`scripts/contract-trunk.ts`): a commit that stages a file outside `contract/domain/`, `contract/vocabulary.md` and `tests/e2e/scenarios/`, added, changed or deleted, a move counted by both its paths, fails, naming each such file and the branch it belongs on. The vocabulary counts whole, so an edit of its grammar there is the review's to see in the trunk's diff; a merge passes, as it brings `main` in with every layer.

The pre-commit hook first refuses a commit while a hook `lefthook.yml` names is not installed (`scripts/hooks-installed.ts`): lefthook installs the hooks on `bunx lefthook install` (or `bun install`), and by itself on a hook run that notices `lefthook.yml` changed (an edit in the same second as the last install can pass unnoticed), unless told not to (`--no-auto-install`), but a hook whose file went while `lefthook.yml` stayed the same stays missing.

### A take: the form of a business commit on a branch

<a id="rule-take-form"></a>
On any other branch but `main` and `feat/contract-first-pass` (an experiment's aside), the commit-msg hook holds a commit that changes the business layer to a take's form (`scripts/contract-take.ts`): its subject `<type>(contract): <the card's goal>`, the business layer alone, and as its last paragraph `Taken-from: contract/trunk <first>^..<last> <card>`, each commit or run of them oldest first, on the trunk's own line, changing every file it stages; a business commit the rules let the branch write itself (the last one of a rename or a removal, a feature begun there before the trunk, an amendment allowed there) names its cards in `Written-on-branch: <card>` instead. Nothing else there names the `contract` scope, so `git log main..<branch> -E --grep='^[a-z]*\(contract\):'` lists the branch's contract changes (`main..` leaves out the meta rounds `main` brings in) and `--grep='^Taken-from:'` its takes. A plain cherry-pick or rebase runs no hook and a revert of a take fails it.

The check reads the `git commit` that runs the hook from `/proc`, its options parsed as git parses them: an amend is judged as the whole commit it leaves, and the message as git records it, cut at its scissors line under `--verbose` and its comment lines stripped or kept by the cleanup mode; where git's default mode leaves that to whether an editor opened, a message its comment lines change fails, and so does one the comment characters git may pick under `core.commentChar=auto` read differently. Without `/proc` (another system), an amend is judged by what it adds and the message is cut at a scissors line it holds. A take is checked against `main` and `contract/trunk` as the clone knows them, the remote's when it has no branch of its own.

### A landing on main: the squash of one branch

<a id="rule-landing-form"></a>
On `main` the same hook judges every commit as a landing (`scripts/contract-take.ts`), the squash of one branch, against its parent: `main`'s HEAD, or, for an amend, the commit under the one it replaces. A merge into `main` is refused, as `main` takes only squashes; any other commit carries exactly one `Squashed-from: <branch> (<tip>) [<cards>]` trailer as its last paragraph, the cards optional for the hook (the landing procedure still names the card). The branch must exist by its exact ref, `refs/heads/<branch>`, so a revision such as `feat/me~0` names none; the tip, 7 to 40 hex digits, must be that branch's tip or one of its ancestors, and a short id git cannot tell from another commit is refused. The parent must lie under the tip, and the committed tree must equal the tip's tree and differ from the parent's, so a landing is `git merge --squash <branch>` and nothing more or less.

Then the branch's round is judged: the commits of the tip that are neither on the parent nor under the tip of an earlier landing's trailer, merges left out, each against the take rules above as git recorded it, its message and the files it changes. A recorded commit of the round that breaks the take rules stops the squash even when its own hook was skipped (a plain cherry-pick, a `--no-verify` commit). The refusal names each commit that fails and how to rewrite it; the check grants no waiver. `contract/trunk` and an experiment never land, an experiment being split into area branches first. The engine's branch, `feat/contract-first-pass`, is bound by the trailer like any other branch, but its round is not judged, as its meta commits carry the `contract` scope with the engine's own files. `bun run scripts/contract-take.ts --round [<branch>]` prints that verdict for a branch (the checked-out one by default) without writing anything, so it runs before the gates; it ends with `contract take: every commit of <branch> since its last landing (<n>) is in its form` when every commit of the round is.

The check has limits. It sees a commit only where git runs the commit-msg hook for it: a change of `main` that runs none is not seen, a fast-forward, a plain cherry-pick or revert, a rebase, or a commit made with `--no-verify`, the squash's own included. `main` changes only by a squash commit, by procedure, and the check cannot force it. A merge's own resolution is not judged, as a merge of the round is left out. Without `/proc` (another system) an amend is not detected, so the commit is judged against HEAD, and an amend that adds no change to `main` is refused as not changing it. An earlier landing's tip that the clone lacks cannot bound the round: its commits are judged again, and a refusal names those tips, so that the branch which landed them can be fetched. An amend of a landing is made before its merge back into the branch: after it, the replaced landing falls into the round and is judged there.

### The manifest: what it holds, how it is built and where it is served

<a id="rule-manifest"></a>
`bun run contract:manifest` (`ui-contract manifest --out <folder>/ui-manifest`) builds `ui-manifest/manifest.json` from the contract files and the scenarios of `tests/e2e/scenarios/` alone, only after the contract check passes: `emitManifest(root, outDir)` runs the contract check, writes nothing and returns its problems when it finds any, else loads the contract and builds, validates and writes the manifest. `buildManifest` takes only the vocabulary, the screens, the model, the activities, the scenarios and the product. `writeManifest(root, outDir, manifest)` validates it against `contract/schemas/manifest.schema.json` with Ajv and returns the problems without writing anything, or empties `outDir` (its last segment must be `ui-manifest`, `MANIFEST_DIR`, else a problem) and writes `manifest.json` (two spaces deep, each entry of `ids` on one line, so a search for an anchor shows its facts), `manifest.schema.json` and `model.schema.json` beside it. `productInfo(root)` names the product: the root package's version and the commit checked out, or null.

The web build writes it into `apps/web/dist/ui-manifest/` (`bun run build` runs `ui-contract manifest --out dist/ui-manifest` once Vite is done; the development watcher writes it into `apps/web/dist-dev/ui-manifest/` after every build, and a change of a contract file or of a scenario rebuilds), and both stands serve it at `/ui-manifest/manifest.json`, with `manifest.schema.json` and `model.schema.json` beside it and `Cache-Control: no-store`. `PERCH_UI_MANIFEST=0` on a stand answers 404 there; the default, `1`, serves it.

It is what a tool outside the repository reads (its keys are the Manifest section of this reference): the vocabulary as tables with prose `instructions` for an agent (`instructionsOf`, its fixed texts read from the rules of this reference, the anchors and states taken from the vocabulary and the platform section); `derivedParts`, each derived suffix with the kind of the part it marks, the meaning of the suffix as its `description` and, for a `visibility-button`, its values; `composites`, the vocabulary's table of the platform's composites, whose declarations are under `platform.composites`; `fieldStates`, the table of field states, `{}` when the vocabulary has none; the screens verbatim, each binding with its `example` link and each anchor with its description, the written one (a secret input's with `(type password)` appended once) or else the one the engine says (see the rule `engine-descriptions`); the platform section and the activities verbatim; the scenarios parsed; `routeParams`; the model document; and `ids`.

`ids` lists every declared anchor by name with its kind, its screen and its attributes: `holdsInput` of a field, the `fieldStates` of a field, the `marksState: false` of a field that writes it (the app leaves its border uncoloured; its `fieldStates` still hold), a ranked message's `ranked`, a secret input's `composite`, and `derivedFrom` on the anchors a composite derives (`{ composite }` on its list, `{ composite, item }` on an item), one entry per line, so a search for an anchor in the manifest shows its facts. `fieldStatesOf` gives the states of a field: only an `input`, `textarea` or `select` that holds input has any, `unsaved`, and `saved` too when its binding is a model field; each state must be declared in the vocabulary's "Field states" table, so a vocabulary without it gives none; listed only when not empty.

A contract change hands over `bun run contract:diff main` with the reason for each rename or removal (see the rule `hand-over`).

### The page helper and the report of a settled state

<a id="rule-page-helper"></a>
On a development build, and on a production build made with `PERCH_UI_HOOKS=1` (the e2e stand's), `window.__perch.ui.anchors()` (`PAGE_HELPER`, `__perch.ui`) lists the anchors the page renders now, in document order, each with its kind (the last dash-separated word of its last segment), `data-state`, `data-code`, `data-id`, `data-value` and visibility (`visible` is false when the element or one of its ancestors is hidden), with the page's URL (`RenderedPage`, `RenderedAnchor`). `ANCHOR_ATTRIBUTES` names the attributes (`data-testid`, `data-state`, `data-code`, `data-id`, `data-value`), and `window.__perch` is declared on `Window` for any code that imports the types. Without the helper, the names a page renders are still there:

```js
;[...document.querySelectorAll('[data-testid]')].map((element) => element.dataset.testid)
```

An e2e run exposes the binding `window.__perchSettledState` (`SETTLED_STATE`, declared on `Window`), and such a build reports to it each settled state of a screen's machine, once the page has rendered it: the screen (`app-shell` for the frame), the state value (XState's, `SettledValue`) and the page as the helper reads it then (`SettledState`). The harness holds every report to the manifest the stand serves (`settledProblems`, see the rule `settled-state`), and a test fails at its end when a state it passed through broke it.

### The check of a settled state

<a id="rule-settled-state"></a>
`settledProblems(contract, settled)` is the e2e harness's check of a `SettledState` against the manifest (any `SettledContract`: each screen's `id` and `flow`, and `ids` with each anchor's `screen`, `kind`, `derived`, `roles`, `own`, `dataId`, `states`, `with` and `without`): what the page shows once a screen's machine has settled in a state, held to what that state renders (`meta.renders`, read by `renderedAt`). One line per problem; a screen the contract lacks is one. Rendered means visible to the user: an anchor the state does not render is not visible, absent from the page or hidden on it; one it renders is on the page and visible.

Only the anchors of the screen that render only where listed are held, with their derived parts; the anchors every state renders are not (a message shows when it carries a code, and an error when there is one). Two directions are checked. A visible anchor of the screen that the state does not render is reported (`is visible, but the state does not render it`), whatever the anchor is: this one is never waived, so an anchor with `roles`, `own` or a `dataId` that is visible in a state that does not render it is reported. An anchor the state renders that is missing from the page, or hidden, is reported (`is not on the page, but the state renders it`, `is hidden, but the state renders it`), unless data decides whether it shows, where the requirement to be present and visible is waived: an anchor with `roles` or `own`, an entry of a list (`dataId`), or an anchor nested under one of these. It is waived as well for the parts a page shows on demand: a select's `option`, a composite's `item`, a field's `error-message`, and a paged list's `range`, `previous-link` and `next-link`, which its read decides; the size select shows wherever the list does and is held.

Beside these, an anchor of the screen that says when it shows is held to its entries (`with`, `without`, see the rule `with-without`), and a visible list to its entries (see the rule `empty-list`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.meta.renders`](#screen-flow-states-name-meta-renders), [`screen.anchors.<name>.roles`](#screen-anchors-name-roles), [`screen.anchors.<name>.own`](#screen-anchors-name-own), [`screen.anchors.<name>.with`](#screen-anchors-name-with), [`screen.anchors.<name>.without`](#screen-anchors-name-without).

### What contract:diff lists of a change

<a id="rule-contract-diff"></a>
`contractAt(ref, root)` reads the contract files, the screen files, the activities and the scenarios at a git ref (`git ls-tree`, `git show`) and loads them, or returns `{ missing: true }` when that commit has no `contract/`; `diffContracts(before, after)` returns the `ContractChanges`, and `bun run contract:diff <ref>` prints them as `formatChanges(changes)` does (see the rule `hand-over`). A contract left undefined is an empty one. The diff lists what both sides write or derive, so a screen written shorter that loads to the same contract shows no change but the descriptions it stops writing.

The lists, in the order of the block, each added and removed and sorted:

- the screens, and the route access lines: who each route serves as `<path> guard <guard>` and `<path> serves <role>`, a route added whole with every line;
- the search parameters of each route, written or derived (the token of a mail link, with its key): a parameter added or removed as `<path> search <name>: <keywords>`, and each keyword it changes as `<path> search <name> <keyword> <before> -> <after>` (`/verify-email search token key "link-invalid" -> "link-expired"`), each side as JSON and `none` where it lacks the keyword. The keywords are the bounds of the value, `default`, `required` (`true`), `key`, `control` and `values` (the control of each enum value); a route added or removed whole lists every parameter, and a description is a description change of its own;
- the anchors, with the anchors renamed (below); the lists that read a platform action as `<anchor> reads platform.<action>`, then each param by name (`q=search:q`, `kind="all"`), then `paged` when it pages: a list moved to another search or to paging shows as its old line removed and its new one added, and a list added whole shows its line;
- on the anchors both sides hold, the derived parts as `<anchor>.<suffix>` (`<input>.visibility-button` for the eye of a password input), the codes as `<anchor> <code>` and the states as `<anchor> <state>`, written or derived, so a constraint added to the model shows as the code it gives and a change of a composite's item state shows on each item it derives, the roles each is shown to as `<anchor> <role>`, an error's fields as `<error> <code> -> <field>`, the fields that hold input as `<anchor> holds input`, the fields that drop their state colour as `<anchor> shows no state colour`, the controls that ask before a destructive action as `<anchor> asks <code>`, the controls that ask before they leave their screen as `<anchor> leaves losing <field>, <field>` (other fields show as the old line removed and the new one added), and the messages that rank their codes as `<anchor> ranks <code> > <code>`, a new order as the old line removed and the new one added, which the codes compared as a set do not show;
- the codes of the contract, the composites by name, the entities and their fields as `<entity>.<field>`, the activities by id;
- the actions of `platform.leaving` added and removed, the entries of `platform.unsavedInput` as `<leave>: <how it asks>` (and `question: <dialog>`), the values of `platform.values` added, removed and changed (see the rule `declared-values`), and the notices of the platform actions as `<action>.<name>`;
- per activity both sides hold, the transitions added and removed as `<state> on <anchor> -> <target>`;
- per screen both sides hold with a flow on either side, the lines of its flow (see the rule `contract-diff-lines`);
- the platform actions by name, and per platform action either side holds the lines of the action (see the rule `contract-diff-lines`);
- per action of the model either side holds, by `<entity>.<action>`, the reads its `changes` names, as `changes <read>`, added and removed (an action one side lacks names none), listed as `model action lines added in <entity>.<action>` (`model action lines added in profile.updateName: changes accounts.list, changes accounts.read`); an action added or removed whole shows each read it changes;
- last, the descriptions changed (see the rule `description-places`).

An anchor removed and one added are a rename wherever a screen and a kind lost exactly one anchor and gained exactly one: with more on either side the pairing would be a guess, so they stay removed and added. What the anchors both sides hold carry (derived parts, codes, states, roles, error fields, input, colour, questions, ranks, descriptions) is compared under a renamed anchor's new name.

`formatChanges(changes)` prints the block a change hands over: `contract changes: none` when nothing changed, else a summary line (`contract changes: 1 anchor renamed`), then one line per list that is not empty (`anchors renamed: device.list.revoke-button -> device.list.sign-out-button`), the transitions as `transitions added in <activity>`, the lines of a flow as `flow lines added in <screen>`, those of a platform action as `platform action lines added in <action>` and those of an action of the model as `model action lines added in <entity>.<action>`, and last `descriptions changed: <place> <before> -> <after>, ...`.

### The lines of a flow and of a platform action in the diff

<a id="rule-contract-diff-lines"></a>
A screen's flow is listed as `flow lines added in <screen id>` and `flow lines removed in <screen id>`, one line per fact the flow says (`flowLines(flow)`, an internal helper of the hand-over).
